\begin{split}\frac{\partial{w}}{\partial{t}}(t,x,d)=&\int{w(t,x^{\prime},d)\,\alpha_{\mathsf{Trans}}(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\frac{\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{\varnothing}(t,x^{\prime})}{\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{\varnothing}(t,x^{\prime})}\,\left(\tfrac{E-\ell_{\mathrm{E}}}{E}\right)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}-\int{w(t,x,d)\,\alpha_{\mathsf{Trans}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{\varnothing}(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}\\
&+\sum_{bd^{\prime}}\left\{\int{w(t,x^{\prime},d^{\prime})\,\alpha_{\mathsf{Trans}}(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\frac{\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{b}(t,x^{\prime})}{\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{b}(t,x^{\prime})}\,\mathbbm{1}_{{d={\sigma}_{\mathrm{IE}}^{b}\mkern-1.0mu{d^{\prime}}}}\,\left(\tfrac{I-\ell_{\mathrm{I}}}{EI}\right)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}\right.\\
&\qquad\qquad\left.-\int{w(t,x,d)\,\alpha_{\mathsf{Trans}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{b}(t,x)\,\mathbbm{1}_{{d^{\prime}={\sigma}_{\mathrm{IE}}^{b}\mkern-1.0mu{d}}}\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}\right\}\\
&+\int{w(t,x^{\prime},d)\,\alpha_{\mathsf{Prog}}(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\frac{\pi_{\mathsf{Prog}}^{\varnothing}(t,x^{\prime})}{\pi_{\mathsf{Prog}}^{\varnothing}(t,x^{\prime})}\,\left(\tfrac{I-\ell_{\mathrm{I}}}{I}\right)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}-\int{w(t,x,d)\,\alpha_{\mathsf{Prog}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\pi_{\mathsf{Prog}}^{\varnothing}(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}\\
&+\sum_{bd^{\prime}}\left\{\int{w(t,x^{\prime},d^{\prime})\,\alpha_{\mathsf{Prog}}(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\frac{\pi_{\mathsf{Prog}}^{b}(t,x^{\prime})}{\pi_{\mathsf{Prog}}^{b}(t,x^{\prime})}\,\mathbbm{1}_{{d={\sigma}_{\mathrm{EI}}^{b}\mkern-1.0mu{d^{\prime}}}}\,\left(\tfrac{1}{I}\right)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}\right.\\
&\qquad\qquad\left.-\int{w(t,x,d)\,\alpha_{\mathsf{Prog}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\pi_{\mathsf{Prog}}^{b}(t,x)\,\mathbbm{1}_{{d^{\prime}={\sigma}_{\mathrm{EI}}^{b}\mkern-1.0mu{d}}}\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}\right\}\\
&+\int{w(t,x^{\prime},d)\,\alpha_{\mathsf{Recov}}(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\mathbbm{1}_{{I\geq\ell_{\mathrm{I}}}}\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}-\int{w(t,x,d)\,\alpha_{\mathsf{Recov}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathbbm{1}_{{I\geq\ell_{\mathrm{I}}}}\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}\\
&+\int{w(t,x^{\prime},d)\,\alpha_{\mathsf{Wane}}(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}-\int{w(t,x,d)\,\alpha_{\mathsf{Wane}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}\\
&-\int{w(t,x,d)\,\alpha_{\mathsf{Sample}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}-\int{w(t,x,d)\,\alpha_{\mathsf{Recov}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathbbm{1}_{{I<\ell_{\mathrm{I}}}}\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}.\end{split}

Here, we have expanded the sums to range over all branches present at time
t
, using the indicator functions to nullify the extra terms.

Table 2.

Elements of the SEIRS model pertinent to the genealogy process.
The population state is
x=(S,E,I,R)
and there are four types of jumps,
x\mapsto{x^{\prime}}=(S^{\prime},E^{\prime},I^{\prime},R^{\prime})
.
For each of these, the table shows the rate (
\alpha_{u}
), production (
r^{u}=(r^{u}_{\mathrm{E}},r^{u}_{\mathrm{I}})
), and event type.

u

\alpha_{u}(t,x,x^{\prime})

r^{u}

Event type

\mathsf{Trans}

\frac{\beta SI}{N}\,\mathbbm{1}_{{S^{\prime}=S-1,E^{\prime}=E+1}}

(1,1)

pure birth

\mathsf{Prog}

\sigma\,E\,\mathbbm{1}_{{E^{\prime}=E-1,I^{\prime}=I+1}}

(0,1)

pure migration

\mathsf{Recov}

\gamma\,I\,\mathbbm{1}_{{I^{\prime}=I-1,R^{\prime}=R+1}}

(0,0)

pure death

\mathsf{Wane}

\omega\,R\,\mathbbm{1}_{{S^{\prime}=S+1,R^{\prime}=R-1}}

(0,0)

neutral

\mathsf{Sample}

\psi\,I\,\mathbbm{1}_{{x^{\prime}=x}}

(0,1)

pure sample

), one recognizes them as a filter equation on the
(x,u,d)
-space, with driver
\beta
, boost
B
, and decay
\lambda
, as follows.

(45)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta(t,x,&x^{\prime},u,u^{\prime},d,d^{\prime})=\alpha_{\mathsf{Trans}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\left(\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{\varnothing}(t,x)\,\mathbbm{1}_{{d^{\prime}=d}}+\sum_{b}{\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{b}(t,x)\,\mathbbm{1}_{{d^{\prime}={\sigma}_{\mathrm{IE}}^{b}\mkern-1.0mu{d}}}}\right)\,\mathbbm{1}_{{u^{\prime}=\mathsf{Trans}}}\\
&+\alpha_{\mathsf{Prog}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\left(\pi_{\mathsf{Prog}}^{\varnothing}(t,x)\,\mathbbm{1}_{{d^{\prime}=d}}+\sum_{b}{\pi_{\mathsf{Prog}}^{b}(t,x)\,\mathbbm{1}_{{d^{\prime}={\sigma}_{\mathrm{EI}}^{b}\mkern-1.0mu{d}}}}\right)\,\mathbbm{1}_{{u^{\prime}=\mathsf{Prog}}}\\
&+\alpha_{\mathsf{Recov}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathbbm{1}_{{I>\ell_{\mathrm{I}}}}\,\mathbbm{1}_{{d^{\prime}=d}}\,\mathbbm{1}_{{u^{\prime}=\mathsf{Recov}}}+\alpha_{\mathsf{Wane}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathbbm{1}_{{d^{\prime}=d}}\,\mathbbm{1}_{{u^{\prime}=\mathsf{Wane}}}\\
&+\mathbbm{1}_{{u^{\prime}=\mathsf{Sample}}}\,\sum_{e\in{S_{0}}}{\mathbbm{1}_{{b_{e}\in\mathsf{col}_{\mathrm{I}}({d})}}\,\mathbbm{1}_{{d^{\prime}={\chi}_{\mathrm{I}}^{b_{e}}\mkern-1.0mu{d}}}\,\delta(e,t)}+\mathbbm{1}_{{u^{\prime}=\mathsf{Sample}}}\,\sum_{e\in{S_{1}}}{\mathbbm{1}_{{b_{e}\in\mathsf{col}_{\mathrm{I}}({d})}}\,\mathbbm{1}_{{d^{\prime}={\chi}_{\mathrm{II}}^{a_{e}b_{e}}\mkern-1.0mu{d}}}\,\delta(e,t)}\\
&+\mathbbm{1}_{{u^{\prime}=\mathsf{Trans}}}\,\sum_{e\in{B}}{\mathbbm{1}_{{b_{e}\cup{b^{\prime}_{e}}\in\mathsf{col}_{\mathrm{I}}({d})}}\,\left[\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{b_{e}b_{e}^{\prime}}(t,x)\,\mathbbm{1}_{{d^{\prime}={\kappa}_{\mathrm{IIE}}^{b_{e}b_{e}^{\prime}}\mkern-1.0mu{d}}}+\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{b_{e}^{\prime}b_{e}}(t,x)\,\mathbbm{1}_{{d^{\prime}={\kappa}_{\mathrm{IIE}}^{b_{e}^{\prime}b_{e}}\mkern-1.0mu{d}}}\right]\,\delta(e,t)}.\\
\end{split}

(46)

\displaystyle\begin{split}B(t,x,&x^{\prime},u,u^{\prime},d,d^{\prime})=\mathbbm{1}_{{u^{\prime}=\mathsf{Trans}}}\,\left(\tfrac{E^{\prime}-\ell_{\mathrm{E}}^{\prime}}{E^{\prime}}\,\tfrac{\mathbbm{1}\{{d^{\prime}=d}\}}{\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{\varnothing}(t,x)}+\tfrac{I^{\prime}-\ell_{\mathrm{I}}^{\prime}}{E^{\prime}I^{\prime}}\,\sum_{b}{\tfrac{\mathbbm{1}\{{d^{\prime}={\sigma}_{\mathrm{IE}}^{b}\mkern-1.0mu{d}}\}}{\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{b}(t,x)}}\right)\\
&+\mathbbm{1}_{{u^{\prime}=\mathsf{Prog}}}\,\left(\tfrac{I^{\prime}-\ell_{\mathrm{I}}^{\prime}}{I^{\prime}}\,\tfrac{\mathbbm{1}\{{d^{\prime}=d}\}}{\pi_{\mathsf{Prog}}^{\varnothing}(t,x)}+\tfrac{1}{I^{\prime}}\,\sum_{b}{\tfrac{\mathbbm{1}\{{d^{\prime}={\sigma}_{\mathrm{EI}}^{b}\mkern-1.0mu{d}}\}}{\pi_{\mathsf{Prog}}^{b}(t,x)}}\right)+\mathbbm{1}_{{u^{\prime}=\mathsf{Recov}}}+\mathbbm{1}_{{u^{\prime}=\mathsf{Wane}}}\\
&+\alpha_{\mathsf{Sample}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathbbm{1}_{{u^{\prime}=\mathsf{Sample}}}\,\left(\sum_{e\in{S_{0}}}{\mathbbm{1}_{{b_{e}\in\mathsf{col}_{\mathrm{I}}({d})}}\,\left(\tfrac{I^{\prime}-\ell_{\mathrm{I}}^{\prime}}{I^{\prime}}\right)\,\mathbbm{1}_{{t=e}}}+\sum_{e\in{S_{1}}}{\mathbbm{1}_{{b_{e}\in\mathsf{col}_{\mathrm{I}}({d})}}\,\tfrac{1}{I^{\prime}}\,\mathbbm{1}_{{t=e}}}\right)\\
&+\alpha_{\mathsf{Trans}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathbbm{1}_{{u^{\prime}=\mathsf{Trans}}}\,\sum_{e\in{B}}{\mathbbm{1}_{{b_{e}\cup{b^{\prime}_{e}}\in\mathsf{col}_{\mathrm{I}}({d})}}\,\tfrac{1}{E^{\prime}I^{\prime}}\,\left[\tfrac{\mathbbm{1}\{{d^{\prime}={\kappa}_{\mathrm{IIE}}^{b_{e}b_{e}^{\prime}}\mkern-1.0mu{d}}\}}{\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{b_{e}b_{e}^{\prime}}(t,x)}+\tfrac{\mathbbm{1}\{{d^{\prime}={\kappa}_{\mathrm{IIE}}^{b_{e}^{\prime}b_{e}}\mkern-1.0mu{d}}\}}{\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{b_{e}^{\prime}b_{e}}(t,x)}\right]\,\mathbbm{1}_{{t=e}}}.\\
\end{split}

(47)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lambda(t,x,&d)=\int{\alpha_{\mathsf{Sample}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}+\int{\alpha_{\mathsf{Recov}}(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathbbm{1}_{{I\leq\ell_{\mathrm{I}}}}\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}.\\
\end{split}

One sees, in particular, that the importance-sampling kernels
\pi
represent the probabilities of changing color on a particular branch (or not doing so), contingent on the population event.
For example,
\pi_{\mathsf{Prog}}^{b}(t,x)
is the probability of proposing an
\mathrm{E}\to\mathrm{I}
change on branch
b
at time
t
given state
x
, given that a
\mathsf{Prog}
event occurs at that time.
The complementary probability
\pi_{\mathsf{Prog}}^{\varnothing}(t,x)
is that of proposing no change to any branch.
As another example
\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{bb^{\prime}}(t,x)
is the probability of proposing, at a genealogical branch-point, that branch
b
is put into deme
\mathrm{I}
and
b^{\prime}
is put into
\mathrm{E}
;

\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{b^{\prime}b}
is the probability of the alternative.
One has wide latitude in the choice of these kernels and by tuning them, one can attempt to minimize Monte Carlo variance.
In particular, one can exploit the permitted time-dependence of the kernels
\pi
so as to “borrow” information from future events.
Thus, for example, since in the SEIR model, branch points can only occur when the parent is in the
\mathrm{I}
deme, one might posit
\pi_{\mathsf{Prog}}
and
\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}
in such a way as to drive the color along a branch into
\mathrm{I}
as it approaches a branch point.

For illustrative purposes, we here use the simpler,
naïve
choice, which is non-anticipatory and always available.
The naïve choice consists of simply proposing changes in proportion to their relative abundance.
Thus, in this case,
\pi_{\mathsf{Prog}}^{b}=\tfrac{1}{E}
for all
b\in\mathsf{col}_{\mathrm{E}}({y})
and
\pi_{\mathsf{Prog}}^{\varnothing}=\tfrac{E-\ell_{\mathrm{E}}}{E}
.
Similarly,
\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{b}=\tfrac{1}{I}
and
\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{\varnothing}=\tfrac{I-\ell_{\mathrm{I}}}{I}
.
At branch points, the two possibilities are given equal probabilities, i.e.,
\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{bb^{\prime}}=\pi_{\mathsf{Trans}}^{b^{\prime}b}=\tfrac{1}{2}
.

6.  Discussion

The theory presented here represents a generalization of the existing coalescent and birth-death-process approaches to phylodynamic inference.
Importantly, it allows computation of the likelihood via strictly forward-in-time computations and permits consideration of models with discrete population structure.
Moreover, inasmuch as the formulae of

english
\msg_info:nne
zref-cleverlangfile-loadedenglish
\tl_put_left:Ne
??
can be efficiently computed via sequential Monte Carlo, explicit expressions for transition probabilities are not needed:
it is sufficient to be able to simulate from the population process.
This feature of the algorithms—known as the
plug-and-play property

(
He et al. 2010
)
—further expands the class of population models that can be confronted with data.

Specifically, the theory gives us the freedom to choose models with many demes.

Volz 2012
and
Rasmussen et al. 2014
have shown a path to phylodynamic inference in the presence of discrete population structure of the kind we consider here.
Their procedures involve a structured-coalescent approximation whereby deme-specific coalescent rates obey a set of differential equations solved backward in time.
The theory presented here avoids the need for such approximations, though in practice whether the exact results are worth their computational cost will depend on both the questions asked and the data.
In particular, even if cheaper approximations are used in data analysis, an exact calculation is invaluable in that it allows one to evaluate the accuracy of proposed approximations, which might otherwise be difficult to establish.

Some existing methods put rather severe limits on the form of the sampling model and, as
Volz & Frost 2014
pointed out, misspecification of the sampling model can lead to large inferential biases.
With the theory presented here, essentially arbitrary specification of the sampling model is possible.
In particular, one can posit sampling at a rate which is an arbitrary function of time and state and include discrete sampling events as well.
It is also possible to condition on the existence of samples.

If sequential Monte Carlo algorithms are used to compute the likelihoods of

, then it is straightforward to simultaneously assimilate information from both time-series and genealogical data.
One can therefore supplement traditional incidence, disease, or mortality time series with genealogical data to improve inference.

A limitation of the theory is that the population models are assumed to be pure jump processes, which allows consideration of demographic stochasticity and environmental stochasticity modeled by jumps involving multiple individuals
(
Bretó & Ionides 2011
)
, but disallows stochastic processes with a diffusive component.
However, we anticipate that it will be possible to incorporate the full range of Markovian environmental stochasticity via extension of this theory to population models containing both diffusion and jump components.

Similarly, the theory presented here assumes that at most one birth event can occur at a time and that migration and sampling events involve at most one source deme at a time (though multiple migrators or samples are permitted).
These restrictions are not essential, and the proof of

, the computational complexity scales linearly with the number of genomic samples.

—it scales super-linearly with population size in general.

.

will in general require effort that is exponential in the number of demes.
For models with many demes, therefore, approaches for ameliorating or circumventing this curse of dimensionality may be necessary.
Critically, the substantial freedom one has in the choice of the importance-sampling kernel
\pi
can be exploited for this purpose.
In particular, since it is permissible to employ an importance sampling distribution adapted to future observations, there is hope for highly efficient algorithmic computation.

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem 1

We are given a pruned genealogy
P=(T,Z,Y)
and a history
H
and wish to compute
\mathsf{Prob}\left[{\boldsymbol{P}_{T}=P}\;\middle|\;{\boldsymbol{H}_{T}=H}\right]
.
First, note that if
\mathsf{ev}({H})\nsupseteq\mathsf{ev}({P})
, then
H
and
P
are incompatible and
\mathsf{Prob}\left[{\boldsymbol{P}_{T}=P}\;\middle|\;{\boldsymbol{H}_{T}=H}\right]=0
.
Similarly, if any event of
H
is incompatible with the local structure of
P

, then necessarily

In either case, the conclusion of the theorem follows.
Let us therefore suppose that neither of these conditions hold.

We can always construct a time-respecting, but otherwise arbitrary ordering of the sample nodes.
Let
\hat{P}_{j}
represent the embedded chain of the pruned genealogy process, i.e., the genealogy subtended by the first
j
samples in this ordering.
Since each
\hat{P}_{j}
contains
\hat{P}_{j^{\prime}}
for all
j^{\prime}<j
,
\hat{P}_{j}
is a Markov chain.
Therefore, we have the factorization

\mathsf{Prob}\left[{P}\;\middle|\;{H}\right]=\prod_{j}{\mathsf{Prob}\left[{\hat{P}_{j}}\;\middle|\;{\hat{P}_{j-1},H}\right]}.

Each factor,
\mathsf{Prob}\left[{\hat{P}_{j}}\;\middle|\;{\hat{P}_{j-1},H}\right]
, can itself be factorized.
In particular, suppose
H
and
\hat{P}_{j-1}
are given and suppose that it is the
K
-th event in
H
that introduces the
j
-th sample.
The
j
-th lineage extends backward in time until it either coalesces with
\hat{P}_{j-1}
or reaches
t=0
(

).
Moreover, since
P
is compatible with
H
, nothing can happen to this lineage between events of
H
.
Therefore, we proceed backward, event by event, as follows.
Since the type of event
K
is given, the color of the
j
-th lineage just prior to that event is known.
At each subsequent event, the lineage either emerges from the event or does not.
Moreover, if the lineage does emerge from the event, the color prior to the event is determined and the color remains unchanged if it does not.
Suppose that lineage
j
is in deme
i
just to the right of event
k<K
and that the deme-
i
occupancy and production are
n_{i}
,
r_{i}
, respectively.
Let
\ell_{ij}
,
s_{ij}
be the lineage count and saturation at this event in
\hat{P}_{j-1}
.
Then the new lineage can be any one of the
n_{i}-\ell_{i}
lineages as yet unaccounted for.
Of these,
r_{i}-s_{ij}
emerge from the event.
Therefore, the probability that lineage
j
emerges from the event is
(r_{i}-s_{ij})/(n_{i}-\ell_{ij})
and the probability that it does not is
(n_{i}-r_{i}-\ell_{ij}+s_{ij})/(n_{i}-\ell_{ij})
.
Let
q_{jk}
be the former if lineage
j
does in fact emerge from the event and the latter if it does not.
Then
\mathsf{Prob}\left[{\hat{P}_{j}}\;\middle|\;{\hat{P}_{j-1},H}\right]=\prod_{k}{q_{jk}}
and
\mathsf{Prob}\left[{P}\;\middle|\;{H}\right]=\prod_{jk}{q_{jk}}
.

Embedded chain of the pruned genealogy process.

Three successive states of the embedded chain of the pruned genealogy process are shown.
At each step, a new lineage is added in a sampling event.
This lineage extends backward until it either coalesces with the pre-existing genealogy or reaches
t=0
.

is derived by computing the probability that it coalesces at each possible point, conditional on the history of the population process.

Now fix
k
and consider the cumulative product
\prod_{m=1}^{j}{q_{mk}}
.
Clearly
\ell_{1,k}=s_{1,k}=0
and, if lineage
j
has not already coalesced to the right of
k
, then
\ell_{j+1,k}=\ell_{j,k}+1
.
Moreover, if
j
emerges from
k
, then
s_{j+1,k}=s_{j,k}+1
and
s_{j+1,k}=s_{j,k}
if it does not.
Therefore, as we accumulate each successive lineage
j
, we move one step to the right in the following diagram.

(1,0)

\frac{n-r}{n-2}
\frac{r-2}{n-2}
\frac{r-s+1}{n-\ell+1}
\frac{n-r-\ell+s}{n-\ell+1}

At each step, the lineage count increases by 1 and the saturation either increases by a unit or stays the same.
The probability of each option is indicated on the corresponding arrow.
Although there are multiple paths by which the process may arrive at the final lineage count and saturation, the product of the probabilities along each path is the same:

\frac{\tbinom{n_{i}-\ell_{i}}{r_{i}-s_{i}}}{\tbinom{n_{i}}{r_{i}}}.

Moreover, since each of the options is independent of the others,

\prod_{j}{q_{jk}}={\scriptscriptstyle\begin{pmatrix}{n}&{\ell}\\
{r}&{s}\end{pmatrix}},

the latter being the binomial ratio defined in

.

Alternatively, one can reason as follows.
Conditional on
\boldsymbol{H}_{T}=H
, at each time
t\in\mathsf{ev}({H})
, a jump of mark
U_{t}
occurred, with a production of
r^{U_{t}}=(r_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{D}}
, resulting in a deme-occupancy of
n(X_{t})=(n_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{D}}
.
In
P
, at time
t
, there are
\ell_{i}=\ell_{i}(Y_{t})
lineages in deme
i
, of which
s_{i}=s_{i}(Y_{t})
are emergent.
By assumption, at each genealogical event, lineages within a deme are exchangeable:
each has an identical probability of being involved.
This exchangeability implies that each lineage present in a deme at time
t
was equally likely to have been one of the emergent lineages.
In particular, at time
t
, the probability that
s_{i}
of the
\ell_{i}
deme-
i
lineages were among the
r_{i}
of
n_{i}
lineages emergent in the unpruned genealogy process is the same as the probability that, upon drawing
\ell_{i}
balls without replacement from an urn containing
r_{i}
red balls and
n_{i}-r_{i}
black balls, exactly
s_{i}
of the drawn balls are red, namely

\frac{{\scriptscriptstyle\begin{pmatrix}{n_{i}-\ell_{i}}\\
{r_{i}-s_{i}}\end{pmatrix}}\,{\scriptscriptstyle\begin{pmatrix}{\ell_{i}}\\
{s_{i}}\end{pmatrix}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\begin{pmatrix}{n_{i}}\\
{r_{i}}\end{pmatrix}}}.

Because the lineages are labeled, each of the
\tbinom{\ell_{i}}{s_{i}}
equally probable sets of
s_{i}
lineages is distinct;
just one of these is the one present in
P
.
Moreover, since, again conditional on
\boldsymbol{H}_{T}=H
, the identities of the lineages involved in a genealogical event are random and independent of the identities selected at all other events, we have established that

\mathsf{Prob}\left[{\boldsymbol{P}_{T}=P}\;\middle|\;{\boldsymbol{H}_{T}=H}\right]=\prod_{t\in\mathsf{ev}({H})}{{\scriptscriptstyle\begin{pmatrix}{n(X_{t})}&{\ell(Y_{t})}\\
{r^{U_{t}}}&{s(Y_{t})}\end{pmatrix}}}.

Returning to the possibility that
H
is incompatible with
P
, since
\mathsf{Prob}\left[{\boldsymbol{P}_{T}=P}\right]=0
if either any
Q_{U_{t}}=0
or
\mathsf{ev}({P})\nsubseteq\mathsf{ev}({H})
, we obtain the result.
∎

Appendix B
Filter equations

B1.  Definition

The likelihoods that appear in

\tl_put_left:Ne
??
are integrals over large sets of histories.
As such, explicit expressions for them are not available, and we require mathematical tools to allow us to manipulate these quantities and devise algorithms for their numerical solution.
The
filter equations
we introduce here are suitable for these purposes, and we devote this appendix to exposing their essential properties.
This extremely convenient formalism has, to our knowledge, not been thoroughly exploited in the context we introduce here, though we note their resemblance to the constructions of
Ogata 1978
,
Puri & Tuan 1986
,
Kliemann et al. 1990
, and
Giesecke & Schwenkler 2018
.

Definition
.

Let
\boldsymbol{X}_{t}
be a continuous-time Markov process with KFE

(B1)

\frac{\partial{u}}{\partial{t}}(t,x)=\int{u(t,x^{\prime})\,\beta(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}-\int{u(t,x)\,\beta(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}.

Suppose that
B:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\lambda:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{R}
are are given measurable functions.
Let
S\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
be locally finite (i.e.,
S\cap{[0,t]}
is finite for all
t>0
).
Then the system of equations

(B2)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{w}}{\partial{t}}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\int{w(t,x^{\prime})\,\beta(t,x^{\prime},x)\,B(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}-\int{w(t,x)\,\beta(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}-\lambda(t,x)\,w(t,x),\qquad

\displaystyle t\notin{S},

(B3)

\displaystyle w(t,x)

\displaystyle=\int{\widetilde{w}(t,x^{\prime})\,\beta(t,x^{\prime},x)\,B(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}},\qquad

\displaystyle t\in{S},

is called the
filter equation

driven by

\beta
, with
boost

B
,
decay

\lambda
, and
observed event times

S
.
Without important confusion, the process
\boldsymbol{X}_{t}
can also be said to drive the filter equation.

Remark 4
.

Trivially, a Kolmogorov forward equation is itself a filter equation with boost
1
, decay
0
, and
S=\varnothing
.

B2.  Properties

The following results show how filter equations allow one to integrate over random histories.

Lemma 1
.

Suppose that
B:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is measurable.
Let
\boldsymbol{V}_{t}
be an
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-valued random process satisfying
\boldsymbol{V}_{0}=1
and

\mathbbm{E}\left[{\boldsymbol{V}_{t}}\;\middle|\;{\boldsymbol{H}_{t}=H_{t}}\right]=\prod_{\mathclap{e\;\in\;\mathsf{ev}({H_{t}})}}{B(e,\widetilde{X}_{e},X_{e})}.

Let the family of measures
\lambda_{t}
on
\mathbb{X}
be defined by

\lambda_{t}(\mathcal{E})=\mathbbm{E}\left[{\boldsymbol{V}_{t}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{{\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\in{\mathcal{E}}}\}}\right],

for measurable
\mathcal{E}
, and let
w(t,x)
be the density of
\lambda_{t}
,
i.e.,
\lambda_{t}(\mathrm{d}{x})=w(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}{x}
.
In particular,
\mathbbm{E}\left[{\boldsymbol{V}_{t}}\right]=\lambda_{t}(\mathbb{X})=\int{w(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}{x}}
.
Then
w
satisfies the initial condition
w(0,x)=p_{0}(x)
and the regular filter equation,

(B4)

\frac{\partial{w}}{\partial{t}}=\int{w(t,x^{\prime})\,\alpha(t,x^{\prime},x)\,B(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}-\int{w(t,x)\,\alpha(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}.

Proof.

Since
\boldsymbol{V}_{0}=1
,
\lambda_{0}(\mathcal{E})=\mathsf{Prob}\left[{\boldsymbol{X}_{0}\in\mathcal{E}}\right]
, which implies that
w(0,x)=p_{0}(x)
.
For
t>0
and
\Delta>0
, the expectation can be broken into three terms, according to whether
H_{t}
has zero, one, or more than one event in
\left({t,t+\Delta}\right]
.
Accordingly, as
\Delta\downarrow{0}
,

w(t+\Delta,x)=\left(1-\Delta\,\int{\alpha(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}\right)\,w(t,x)+\Delta\,\int{\alpha(t,x^{\prime},x)\,B(t,x^{\prime},x)\,w(t,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}+o(\Delta).

Forming a difference quotient and taking the limit, we obtain

.
Note that here we use the assumption that
\alpha
and
B
are càdlàg as functions of
t
.
∎

When events are known to have occurred at particular times, it is of interest to integrate over those histories that include an event at each of these times.
This leads to singular filter equations, as the next lemma shows.
Before we state the lemma, some terminology is needed.
Let
\mathbb{S}
be the space of increasing, locally finite sequences in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, with the topology induced by the Skorokhod metric and Lebesgue measure.
For
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
S\in\mathbb{S}
, let
S_{t}\coloneq{S\cap{[0,t]}}
.
Thus if
S\in\mathbb{S}
and
S_{t}=(\hat{s}_{1},\dots,\hat{s}_{K})
, then the infinitesimal element of Lebesgue measure at
S_{t}
is
\mathrm{d}{S_{t}}=\prod_{n=1}^{K}\mathrm{d}{\hat{s}_{n}}
.

Lemma 2
.

Suppose that
B:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is measurable and

\boldsymbol{V}_{t}
is an
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-valued random process satisfying

\mathbbm{E}\left[{\boldsymbol{V}_{t}}\;\middle|\;{\boldsymbol{H}_{t}=H_{t}}\right]=\prod_{\mathclap{e\;\in\;\mathsf{ev}({H_{t}})}}{B(e,\widetilde{X}_{e},X_{e})}.

Let
\lambda_{t}
be a family of measures on
\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{S}
defined by

\lambda_{t}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{S})=\mathbbm{E}\left[{\boldsymbol{V}_{t}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{{\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\in{\mathcal{E}}}\}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{{\exists{S\in\mathcal{S}}\>\text{s.t.}\>\mathsf{ev}({\boldsymbol{H}_{t}})\supseteq{S_{t}}}\}}\right],

whenever
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathbb{X}
and
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{S}
are measurable.
Let
w(t,x,S)
be the density of this measure, i.e.,

\lambda_{t}(\mathrm{d}{x}\,\mathrm{d}{S})=w(t,x,S)\,\mathrm{d}{x}\,\mathrm{d}{S_{t}}.

Then
w
satisfies

(B5)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{w}}{\partial{t}}(t,x,S)

\displaystyle=\int{w(t,x^{\prime},S)\,\alpha(t,x^{\prime},x)\,B(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}-\int{w(t,x,S)\,\alpha(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}},\qquad

\displaystyle t\notin{S},

(B6)

\displaystyle w(t,x,S)

\displaystyle=\int{\widetilde{w}(t,x^{\prime},S)\,\alpha(t,x^{\prime},x)\,B(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}},\qquad

\displaystyle t\in{S}.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction on the cardinality of
S_{t}
.
The base case, for which
S_{t}=\varnothing
, follows immediately from

B3.  Adjoint filter equation

Given driver, boost, and decay functions, a filter equation determines a family of measures on
\mathbb{X}
.
Specifically, suppose that, for
s\leq{t}
and
S\subset{\mathbb{R}}
locally finite,
w(s,x,t,x^{\prime})
satisfies the filter equation

(B7)

\displaystyle w(s,x,s,x^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{{x^{\prime}=x}}

(B8)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{w}}{\partial{t}}(s,x,t,x^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\int{w(s,x,t,\xi)\,\beta(t,\xi,x^{\prime})\,B(t,\xi,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{\xi}}

\displaystyle\qquad-\int{w(s,x,t,x^{\prime})\,\beta(t,x^{\prime},\xi)\,\mathrm{d}{\xi}}-\lambda(t,x^{\prime})\,w(s,x,t,x^{\prime}),\qquad

\displaystyle t\notin{S},

(B9)

\displaystyle w(s,x,t,x^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\int{\widetilde{w}(s,x,t,\xi)\,\beta(t,\xi,x^{\prime})\,B(t,\xi,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{\xi}},\qquad

\displaystyle t\in{S}.

Let
\omega
be the measure whose density is
w
:

\omega(s,x,t,\mathcal{E})\coloneq\int_{\mathcal{E}}{w(s,x,t,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}.

If
f:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{R}
is a measurable function, then one defines its integral with respect to this measure in the usual way:

\omega(s,x,t,f)=\int{\omega(s,x,t,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}})\,f(x^{\prime})}=\int{w(s,x,t,x^{\prime})\,f(x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}.

In particular,
\omega(s,x,t,f)
is itself a measurable function of
x
and we have the Chapman-Kolmogorov-like relation

(B10)

\omega(s,x,t,f)=\int{\omega(s,x,\tau,\omega(\tau,\xi,t,f))\,\mathrm{d}{\xi}}=\int{\omega(s,x,\tau,\mathrm{d}{\xi})\,\omega(\tau,\xi,t,f)},

whenever
s\leq{\tau}\leq{t}
.
Let us now fix
t
and
f
and write
F(s,x)=\omega(s,x,t,f)
.
Let us take
\beta
,
B
, and
\lambda
to be càdlàg in
t
and
F
to be càglàd in
s
.
Applying

we obtain, for

\begin{split}F(s-\Delta,x)=&\left(1-\Delta\,\int{\beta(s-\Delta,x,\xi)\,\mathrm{d}{\xi}}-\Delta\,\lambda(s-\Delta,x)\right)\,F(s,x)\\

\begin{split}-\frac{\partial{F}}{\partial{s}}&=\int{\widetilde{\beta}(s,x,\xi)\,\left[\widetilde{B}(s,x,\xi)\,F(s,\xi)-F(s,x)\right]\,\mathrm{d}{\xi}}-\widetilde{\lambda}(s,x)\,F(s,x).\end{split}

, we have

denotes the right-limit of

are satisfied by
w(t,x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{v\,u(t,x,v)\,\mathrm{d}{v}}
, where

satisfies the KFE

(B13)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{u}}{\partial{t}}(t,x,v)=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial{v}}\left[\lambda(t,x)\,v\,u(t,x,v)\right]+\int_{0}^{\infty}\int{u(t,x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\,\beta(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\delta\!\left(B(t,x^{\prime},x)\,v^{\prime},v\right)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}{v^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}\int{u(t,x,v)\,\beta(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\delta\!\left(B(t,x,x^{\prime})\,v,v^{\prime}\right)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}{v^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle t\notin{S},

\displaystyle u(t,x,v)\,\mathrm{d}{x}=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int{\widetilde{u}(t,x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\,\pi(t,x^{\prime},\mathrm{d}{x})\,\delta\!\left(A(t,x^{\prime})\,B(t,x^{\prime},x)\,v^{\prime},v\right)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}{v^{\prime}}},

A(t,x)\coloneq{\int{\beta(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}}

and
\pi(t,x,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}})\coloneq\beta(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}/A(t,x)

Proof.

For each
t\notin{S}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{w}}{\partial{t}}(t,x)=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{v\,\frac{\partial{u}}{\partial{t}}(t,x,v)\,\mathrm{d}{v}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int\int_{0}^{\infty}{v\,u(t,x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\,\beta(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\delta\!\left(B(t,x^{\prime},x)\,v^{\prime},v\right)\,\mathrm{d}{v}\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}{v^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}\int\int_{0}^{\infty}{v\,u(t,x,v)\,\beta(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\delta\!\left(B(t,x,x^{\prime})\,v,v^{\prime}\right)\,\mathrm{d}{v}\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}{v^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}{v\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial{v}}\left[\lambda(t,x)\,v\,u(t,x,v)\right]\,\mathrm{d}{v}}.

Here, the non-explosivity assumption guarantees that we can differentiate under the integral sign and exchange the order of integration.
Moreover, it ensures that
u\to{0}
as
v\to{\infty}
.
Hence, by evaluating the first integral with respect to
v
, the second with respect to
v^{\prime}
, and the third by parts, we obtain

\frac{\partial{w}}{\partial{t}}(t,x)=\int{v^{\prime}\,u(t,x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\,\beta(t,x^{\prime},x)\,B(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\mathrm{d}{v^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}-\int{v\,u(t,x,v)\,\beta(t,x,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{v}\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}-\lambda(t,x)\,\int{v\,u(t,x,v)\,\mathrm{d}{v}},

which is simplified to obtain

, we have

\displaystyle w(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}{x}=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int\int_{0}^{\infty}{v\,\widetilde{u}(t,x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\,\pi(t,x^{\prime},\mathrm{d}{x})\,\delta\!\left(A(t,x^{\prime})\,B(t,x^{\prime},x)\,v^{\prime},v\right)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}{v^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}{v}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int{v^{\prime}\,\widetilde{u}(t,x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\,\pi(t,x^{\prime},\mathrm{d}{x})\,A(t,x^{\prime})\,B(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}{v^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\widetilde{w}(t,x^{\prime})\,\beta(t,x^{\prime},x)\,B(t,x^{\prime},x)\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}\,\mathrm{d}{x}.

which is equivalent to

.

\boldsymbol{V}_{t}
is
directed
by
\boldsymbol{X}_{t}
in the sense that
\boldsymbol{V}
has jumps wherever
\boldsymbol{X}
does:
when
\boldsymbol{X}
jumps at time
t
from
x
to
x^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{V}
jumps by the multiplicative factor
B(t,x,x^{\prime})\geq{0}
.
Between jumps,
\boldsymbol{V}_{t}
decays deterministically and exponentially at rate
\lambda(t,x)
.
At the known times in
S
,
\boldsymbol{X}
jumps according to the probability kernel
\pi
and,
\boldsymbol{V}
jumps by the factor
A(t,x)\,B(t,x,x^{\prime})
.
If we view
\boldsymbol{V}_{t}
as a weight, then

is written for the time-homogeneous case, in which neither
\beta(t,x)
nor
\lambda(t,x)
depend explicitly on
t
.
In the time-inhomogeneous case, the exponential waiting-time and weight updates would be replaced by the corresponding integrated-hazard expressions.
This is a simple sequential importance sampling algorithm and as such can be improved upon in many ways
(
Doucet et al. 2001
;
Arulampalam et al. 2002
;
Chopin & Papaspiliopoulos 2020
;
Wills & Schön 2023
)
.
Our purpose here is merely to illustrate the implications of

Sequential Monte Carlo filter.

.
It takes as input an initial time
t_{0}
, a final time
T
, and a sequence of observed event times
S=\left\{{t_{1},\dots,t_{K}}\right\}
.
We order the latter so that
t_{0}\leq{t_{1}}<t_{2}<\cdots<t_{K}<{t_{K+1}}\coloneq{T}
.
In addition, we are given event rates
\beta
, a boost function
B
, a decay function
\lambda
, and the initial distribution of latent states
p_{0}
.
As in

, we let

\begin{gathered}A(x)\coloneq{\int{\beta(x,x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}}},\qquad\pi(x,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}})\coloneq\frac{\beta(x,x^{\prime})}{A(x)}\,\mathrm{d}{x^{\prime}}.\end{gathered}

For a given, fixed ensemble size,
J\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, the algorithm returns an unbiased Monte Carlo estimate,
\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathcal{L}}}
, of the likelihood.

1:

procedure

SMCFilter
(
t_{0},T,S,\beta,B,\lambda,p_{0},J
)

2:

Initialize an ensemble of latent states and weights:

3:

x_{j}\sim{p_{0}(\cdot)}
, for
j=1,\dots,J

4:

w_{j}\leftarrow{1}
, for
j=1,\dots,J

5:

for

k=0,\dots,K

do

\triangleright
loop over observed event times

6:

for

j=1,\dots,J

do

\triangleright
loop over the ensemble

7:

t\leftarrow{t_{k}}

8:

if

k>0

then

\triangleright
singular part (

9:

x^{\prime}_{j}\sim\pi(x_{j},\cdot)

10:

w_{j}\leftarrow{w_{j}\,A(x_{j})\,B(t,x_{j},x^{\prime}_{j})}

11:

x_{j}\leftarrow{x^{\prime}_{j}}

12:

while

t<t_{k+1}

do

regular part (

13:

\Delta\sim{\mathrm{Exp}(A(x_{j}))}

\triangleright
Sojourn times are exponentially distributed.

14:

if

t+\Delta<{t_{k+1}}

then

15:

x^{\prime}_{j}\sim{\pi(x_{j},\cdot)}

16:

t^{\prime}\leftarrow{t+\Delta}

17:

w_{j}\leftarrow{w_{j}\,B(t^{\prime},x_{j},x^{\prime}_{j})\,\exp{(-\lambda(x_{j})\,\Delta)}}

18:

x_{j}\leftarrow{x^{\prime}_{j}}

19:

t\leftarrow{t^{\prime}}

20:

else

21:

\Delta\leftarrow{t_{k+1}-t}

22:

w_{j}\leftarrow{w_{j}\,\exp{(-\lambda(x_{j})\,\Delta)}}

23:

t\leftarrow{t_{k+1}}

24:

\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathcal{L}}}\leftarrow\tfrac{1}{J}\displaystyle\sum_{j}{w_{j}}
